News
OIL FUTURES: Crude slides as market eyes Middle East diplomatic efforts
Diplomatic efforts cool near-term escalation fears US post-election strike risk persists
Russia sends fourth diesel tanker to US
Crude oil futures settled lower Oct. 27 as Middle East diplomatic efforts cooled concerns of near-term regional escalation that could threaten supply.
NYMEX December WTI settled $1.86/barrel lower at $99.90/b and ICE December Brent declined $1.93/b to $113.88/b.
"Renewed Middle East diplomacy is seeking to avert wider US-Iran escalation, with Saudi, Pakistani, and Turkish officials meeting Iranian leaders to revive talks and prevent further USIsraeli strikes. The effort comes before US midterms, as Trump hints strikes could resume," said Saxo APAC Research Oct. 27.
Representatives from Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and Turkey met with Iranian leaders in Tehran on Oct. 26 to discuss the ongoing supply crisis, according to media reports.
Iran's supreme leader, Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei, met with Major General Dr. Mohsen Rezaei, Secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, to discuss bringing the "current state of `neither war nor peace' to an end," according to an Oct. 27 Englishlanguage post by Khamenei on X, formerly known as Twitter.
Market analysts, however, highlight that despite the diplomatic talks, the risk of military escalation remains elevated.
"The risk of renewed US strikes on Iran after the midterm elections continues to support our higher-for-longer scenario," Arne Lohmann Rasmussen, chief analyst at GRM, said Oct. 27 in a report.

Market analysis
International Crude
INTERVIEW: Uganda's new energy minister eyes infrastructure push beyond first oil
First oil due in 2026, exports to start in 2027
Third bid round expected to launch imminently
Dangote joins Kenya Pipeline Company as co-owner
Uganda is on the brink of becoming the world's youngest oil producer by the end of this year, with the launch of the contentious East Africa Crude Oil pipeline set to link the country's oilfields to the world market after almost a decade in the making.
However, the long-awaited pipeline suffered new setbacks in the wake of the US-Iran war, while a massive new Kenyan refinery project has overhauled expected trade routes for the country's oil, its energy minister told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, in an exclusive interview.
Speaking on the sidelines of African Energy Week in Cape Town earlier this month, new energy minister Monica Musenero said that Uganda should achieve first oil from its CNOOCoperated Kingfisher oilfields by the end of 2026, although the first exports will not leave Tanzania until early 2027.
The Ugandan oil project involves two developments, Kingfisher and Tilenga, which are being developed by CNOOC and TotalEnergies, and will rely on the EACOP to shuttle up to 230,000 barrels/day of medium-to-heavy sweet crude to the Tanzanian coast.
However, after years of delays linked to financing and environmental opposition, the pipeline project hit another speedbump when the US-Iran war trapped vital equipment in the Persian Gulf. "We had to airlift," Musenero said, adding that the developers were left with no alternative when parts were left stranded beyond the Strait of Hormuz.
The delay has meant a missed opportunity for Uganda to capitalize on crude prices that have traded above $100/b over the last two months, well above the $60/b it had used as its benchmark.
"I wish I were selling crude now," Musenero said, adding that a long-delayed third licensing round should also be coming soon to encourage new exploration. Discussions are already underway over potential OPEC membership, which the minister sees as a useful partnership for newer producers.
Nevertheless, since assuming the role in June, Musenero, who previously ran the science and technology ministry, has

October 27, 2026

emphasized plans to look beyond the raw material as a future source of growth for the energy sector.
"After about 10 years of crude marketing, Uganda's economy should not be dependent so much on the price of crude, but the value-added things that we are selling," Musenero said, highlighting plans for new refining and logistics capacity to diversify revenues.
In the future, the country could also offer a "green barrel" offset with its forests and wetlands that act as a carbon sink, potentially giving it an edge for climate-conscious lenders, Musenero said.
Downstream plans As part of its downstream drive, the country is developing
a $4-billion refinery in Hoima, which will eventually have the capacity to refine up to 60,000 b/d of crude. With the support of Emirati investor Alpha MBM, Uganda hopes to commission the refinery in roughly 2-4 years, Musenero said.
Beyond that date, the aim is to develop an "oil city" around the country's Kabalenga Industrial Park, she said, which has been earmarked for an integrated petrochemicals facility capable of producing polypropylene and other products.
The Hoima refinery will be given first call on domestic crude produced from the country's Albertine Graben basin, Musenero said, which could reduce the available barrels to be marketed by Vitol to international refiners.
Additionally, new plans by Nigeria's Dangote Group to build a 700,000 b/d refinery in Lamu, Kenya by the end of the decade could keep far more crude within the continent than initially anticipated, overhauling expected trade routes into Asia and beyond.
"When we started discussions and looking at our growth, there

was no Lamu. And now there is Lamu as part of the formula," Musenero said, adding the project has created a stronger impetus to avoid long-term contracts and maintain trade flexibility.
Infrastructure play Uganda has yet to take a final investment decision on a
potential stake in the Dangote refinery and sales of its flagship Pearl Sweet crude will be driven by market dynamics regardless of any state interest in the project, Musenero stressed.
However, the refinery plan appears to have driven a new focus on regional trade links for the wider East African market, with potential upside for Uganda as a regional trade hub. For instance, Dangote recently joined the Uganda National Oil Company as a co-owner of Kenya's state-owned pipeline company, which supplies the majority of Uganda's fuel, but has struggled to keep pace with the growing demands of the inland market.
"We are working actively with Kenya to make that pipeline go all the way to Uganda," Musenero said, commenting that extending the network was on her "urgent list" and undergoing feasibility studies. Dangote plans to invest in the network to link it to its Lamu refinery, offering an inland channel for its products, an executive said.
Building on plans from her predecessor, Musanero is also focused on developing two new oil product pipelines from Tanzania and Kenya, which will be capable of transporting motor fuels and LPG, although no FID has been taken. In the capital, Kampala, builders also began constructing new storage in September, which should increase the country's total capacity to 360 million liters by 2028, Musenero said.
Among others, the country's emerging oil sector has already captured the attention of Vitol, which has floated plans for a new $20 billion storage hub in Tanga, Tanzania, where the EACOP ends.
"We were previously labeled landlocked, so unfavorable for investment," the minister said, emphasizing that new investment has shifted attitudes from Middle Eastern, African and American financiers. "Now, it's being looked at more like when you see that map of East Africa, Uganda is at the heart of it," she said.
-- Kelly Norways
Algeria's Sonatrach lowers Nov Saharan Blend OSP by $7.25/b on month
Nov OSP set at Dated Brent plus $5.75/b
Freight costs trap oil within basin
Algeria's state-owned Sonatrach has lowered the official selling price for Saharan Blend by $7.25/b for November loading cargoes, according to a notice seen Oct. 27 by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Sonatrach set its Saharan Blend OSP for September loading at Dated Brent plus $5.75/b.

differential at $1/b above Dated Brent on Oct. 26, down $4.50/b month over month.
The decline in differentials was driven by narrower refining margins and a well-supplied Mediterranean sweet crude oil market.
"The [Med] market is collapsing," a Mediterranean-based crude trader said Oct. 27, noting that a Sarahan Blend cargo loading in the second half of November was traded at a discount to Dated Brent.
WTI Midland barrels headed toward Europe have been a key source of supply-side pressure on Mediterranean grades.
At the same time, the punitive freight environment in the Mediterranean has trapped barrels within the basin, forcing volumes to be cleared locally.
-- Ernest Puey
CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex sees one convergence
19 Dubai partials trade during MOC
One Dec-loading Oman crude cargo declared
The Middle East sour crude complex saw one convergence during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 27, while cash differentials for key sour crude markers slid for a third day.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai, Oman and Murban all at a premium of $6.17/b to same-month Dubai futures at the market close, down 52 cents/b on the day.
During the MOC, 19 December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were Unipec, Vitol, Gunvor,

Phillips 66, Mercuria and Glencore, while the buyers were Trafigura, Mitsui and BP.
Vitol declared a cargo of December Oman crude to Trafigura following the convergence of 20 partials in Platts cash Dubai.
A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.

2027, a milestone that could significantly reshape commodity trade flows between Russia's Arctic energy projects and Asian markets, a senior company official told Interfax.
The transition to continuous navigation could begin with the nuclear-powered icebreaker Chukotka expected to enter service in early 2027, bringing Russia's nuclear icebreaker fleet to nine vessels, Vladimir Panov, director for Northern Sea Route and Arctic development at Rosatom's Arctic Directorate, said, according to an Interfax report Oct. 27.
"We are now in the final stage of preparation for regular eastbound voyages on the Northern Sea Route," Panov said.
The fleet expansion comes on the heels of Russia's largest oil producer Rosneft beginning shipments from its flagship Vostok Oil project in the Arctic in September.
Rosneft officially launched the project on Sept. 6, when it loaded the first cargo of Vostok oil at the Sever Bay port on the Kara Sea. The first vessel from the project to reach China landed at the port of Yantai on Oct. 19, having traveled by the Northern Sea Route, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
In early November, two more tankers carrying oil from the project are expected to reach China, while another one is destined for India. Loadings at Sever Bay averaged 89,000 b/d in September and 71,000 b/d in October, according to CAS.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed front-month ESPO cargoes supplied via Kozmino in Russia's Far East on a FOB basis at $117.68/b on Oct. 26.
Route value Most supplies from Vostok Oil are expected to travel via the
Northern Sea Route, which has historically had a navigational season typically starting from June-July. The route provides significant time and cost savings compared to alternatives.
According to Panov, overall cargo shipments along the NSR are expected to set a new record of more than 40 million metric tons this year, with transit cargo shipments -- meaning cargo

that passes through the NSR but is not extracted in the Arctic -- almost doubling to a record of over 7 million mt.
Cargo traffic through Russia's Northern Sea Route has increased because of the disruption caused by the conflict in the Middle East, the Ukraine war, and US-China tensions, according to Mikhail Korostikov of the Carnegie Russia Eurasia Center.
"Crucially, the Northern Sea Route is largely free from geopolitical risk," he said on the organization's website Sept. 30. The pace of its development has been in proportion to the problems and delays on other shipping lanes, he added.
Rosatom's Panov said that achieving year-round status for the NSR is only the first step, with convoy speed identified as a key operational challenge.
"Conducting a convoy in the winter-spring period is not a problem. But to improve the efficiency of cargo transportation, convoy speed needs to increase, and this is now one of the main tasks," he said.
Work is needed to expand emergency rescue infrastructure, communications and medical services along the route, he said.
Russia's Ministry of Emergency Situations has established Arctic Emergency Rescue Centers in Pevek and Sabetta, with additional centers under construction in Dikson and Tiksi. The Ministry of Transport has assembled a fleet of 14 emergency rescue vessels for the route, with 10 more under construction, Interfax reported.
-- Thomas Washington, Rosemary Griffin
Eni, TotalEnergies complete Nigerian onshore oil divestments
Eni completes asset transfer to Apex Global
TotalEnergies concludes onshore deal with Vaaris
IOCs shift focus to Nigerian deepwater assets
Italy's Eni and France's TotalEnergies have completed the divestments of a string of Nigerian onshore oil assets, transferring them to indigenous producers, as international oil companies focus on deepwater projects in the African country.
In a statement released late Oct. 26, Eni said it had concluded the sale of its Nigerian subsidiary, Agip Energy and Natural Resources, or AENR, to Apex Global Energy, a company wholly owned by Nigerian firm Sterling Oil Exploration and Energy Production.
The transaction comes two years after Eni sold four Nigerian onshore producing oil blocks - OMLs 60, 61, 62 and 63 - as well as stakes in the Brass terminal, exploration concessions and power plants, to another Nigerian upstream operator, Oando.
The latest divestment sees Eni selling off AENR's interests in onshore and shallow-water oil and gas exploration and production assets in Nigeria, including a 5% stake in the Renaissance joint venture, where it was partnered with TotalEnergies and state-run Nigerian National Petroleum Co.
"The transaction, which has received approval from all relevant authorities, is in line with Eni's strategy to optimize its upstream activities by rebalancing the portfolio and divesting

Malaysia's Sarawak adds 800 b/d of onshore oil output: official
Sarawak producing half of Malaysia's oil
65-km Samalaju pipeline targets 2026 finish
Kuching LNG terminal aims for 2029 startup
Malaysia's Sarawak has brought about 800 barrels/day of onshore oil production online and is advancing gas infrastructure to supply growing industrial and power demand, Deputy Minister of Energy and Environmental Sustainability of Sarawak Dr. Haji Hazland bin Abang Hipni said at the Singapore International Energy Week Oct. 27.
State-owned Petroleum Sarawak, or Petros and Petra Energy, started production from the Adong Kechil West early production system in Block SK433 in Miri in July, reviving the state's onshore

oil production after nearly 50 years, Hazland said. "Sarawak is producing about 300,000 b/d, which is about half
of the oil production in Malaysia," Hazland said. The state is also targeting completion of the 65-kilometer
Samalaju gas pipeline by the end of 2026 to supply industries at the Samalaju Industrial Park, he said. He did not specify the throughput capacity or quantify additional gas production from the upstream developments.
"We are strengthening our energy security by developing our onshore and also our offshore oil and gas resources while expanding hydropower and solar generation," Hazland said.
Sarawak plans further onshore exploration, while Petros is exploring integrated development of offshore gas fields in western Sarawak to provide a reliable supply for a low-carbon industrial hub, he said.
The projects form part of the state's push to increase domestic use of its gas resources, supporting power generation and attracting downstream investment.
Petros said in a July 24 statement that the Adong Kechil West early production system would provide reservoir and operational data to support future development of surrounding discoveries as a cluster.
The next development phase will focus on production facilities and infrastructure to sustain gas supply to the Miri combined-cycle gas turbine power plant, which is targeted for commissioning by the end of 2027, the company said.
LNG supply for southern Sarawak Petros has also entered into a joint development
agreement with Norway-based Höegh Evi for the proposed Kuching LNG terminal, part of the Kuching Low-Carbon Hub, Hazland said.
The project would establish Sarawak's first LNG regasification facility, supplying Kuching and the surrounding areas. Together with western offshore gas resources, it would support longerterm fuel requirements for power generation and industrial activity in southern Sarawak, he said.
Höegh Evi said Sept. 30 that the partners would jointly develop, own and operate the terminal, comprising a floating storage and regasification unit, mooring infrastructure, subsea and onshore pipelines, and receiving facilities.
First gas is targeted by the end of 2029, with the partners assessing opportunities to accelerate delivery, according to the company. Planned services also include small-scale LNG distribution, truck loading, bunkering, storage and reloading.
Hazland said gas-fired generating capacity was being developed in Miri and Samalaju as electricity demand rises. Solar power purchase agreements representing 140 megawatts had also been signed, complementing hydropower, while gas provides firm power to support the energy transition.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December JKM, the benchmark for LNG delivered to Northeast Asia, at $33.123/ million British thermal unit Oct. 26, up $1.192/MMBtu, from the previous assessment. The LNG DES Southeast Asia Marker, or

Indian refiners favor delivered crude in procurement shift as shipping costs mount
HPCL looks to maintain term-spot buying flexibility
BPCL adjusting crude slate to delivered economics
Indian state-run refiners are increasingly seeking crude on a delivered, or CIF, basis rather than on FOB terms, as geopolitical tensions drive sharp swings in freight and insurance costs, petroleum ministry officials and refiners said Oct. 26.
Under CIF arrangements, sellers cover the cost and risk of transporting crude to the buyer's destination. This allows Indian refiners to secure a more predictable landed cost and avoid exposure to sudden increases in freight rates, which have risen sharply amid disruptions and attacks involving vessels in the Gulf region, they added.
"We are buying crude on a delivered basis these days and not on FOB as we used to buy earlier," a senior Indian refinery official told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Oct. 26.
Since freight costs for crude shipments have risen sharply in recent weeks compared with previous months, refiners are increasingly concerned that an apparently attractive purchase could become uneconomical by the time it reaches an Indian refinery, the official added.
Hindustan Petroleum Corp. Ltd. Chairman and Managing Director Vikas Kaushal told an investor call Oct. 23 that the staterun refiner was looking at buying crude on a landed basis, except when it could send a vessel to pick up cargoes at a relatively lower cost.
"It is now a volatile situation, on how the market is reacting," Kaushal said.
Refiners are increasingly comparing delivered crude costs from suppliers worldwide and selecting the lowest landed-cost options, rather than committing to cargoes on an FOB basis and arranging shipping separately, petroleum ministry and refinery officials said.
"Crude costs are higher as freight costs are higher and

cargoes are getting hit in the Middle East," a refinery official said. The scenario also reflects refiners' reluctance to maintain
fixed term-contract and spot-market procurement ratios amid heightened uncertainty, Kaushal said. He added that HPCL was no longer relying on a 50-50 split between term and spot purchases, as geopolitical conditions had materially changed the economics of crude sourcing.
For state-run refiners, high crude and freight costs can squeeze margins and increase the risk of under-recoveries, particularly as they are expected to support cooking gas supplies for lower-income households, refinery officials said.
Venezuelan crude flows Kaushal said HPCL was also undertaking technical initiatives
to synchronize operations at its refineries to process heavy Venezuelan crude grades in the near future.
HPCL Rajasthan Refinery (HRRL) began operations on July 4. Its high complexity allows it to process Venezuelan crude, but the logistics still need to be worked out, said DLN Sastri, former director of refining at the Federation of Indian Petroleum Industry.
Indian companies could play a larger role in Venezuela's energy sector as the country's oil production increases, importing more of its heavy crude for processing while exploring new opportunities to invest in upstream projects, US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Oct. 20.
"Venezuelan crude has traditionally flowed to Reliance Industries, but as production rises, more complex Indian refiners may explore opportunities to process heavier grades, such as Merey-16," said Benjamin Tang, head of liquid bulk at S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
According to CAS data, 12.6 million barrels of Venezuelanorigin crude, including in-transit volumes, are expected to be discharged at Indian ports in October, up from 10.2 million barrels in May, highlighting a sharp near-term increase in flows. Completed inflows of Venezuelan crude into India have averaged 214,000 barrels/day so far this year, with 82% of the volume delivered to Port of Sikka.
Russian flows Another state-run refiner, Bharat Petroleum Corp. Ltd., is also
adjusting its crude slate based on the delivered economics of competing supplies.
BPCL plans to increase Russian crude purchases for December delivery despite elevated freight costs, Director (Finance) V. R. Gupta told an investor call Oct. 26. The refiner's processing of Russian crude stood at about 37% over JulySeptember, largely unchanged from April-June, he added.
"Prevailing freight and insurance rates are still high, although ample crude supplies are available in the global markets," Gupta said.
Russian crude discharges to India rose sharply in the first half of 2026, increasing from 1.17 million b/d in January to a peak of 2.43 million b/d in June, according to CAS data. Flows eased to

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Inpex moves second Dec-loading Ichthys Field Condensate cargo
Chevron's late-Dec loading Wheatstone cargo remains unsold
Middle Eastern producer sells Khuff condensate cargo
Sudan heard to have sold Dec-loading barrels of Nile Blend cargoes
The Asia-Pacific sweet crude market on Oct. 27 saw further trade clarity emerging on both the regional and arbitrage condensate front, while Indonesian refiner Pertamina have rescinded a crude and condensate buy tender for first-quarter 2027 to their refineries at Balikpapan, Balongan, Cilacap and Tuban, market sources said.
In the condensate segment, Japan's INPEX sold its Dec. 22-26 loading barrels of the Australian Ichthys Field Condensate cargo to oil major ExxonMobil, market sources said. However, further details of the trade remained unclear, according to several trade sources.
Earlier in the cycle, France's TotalEnergies sold its Australian Ichthys Field condensate cargo scheduled to load over Dec. 11-15, with initial trade levels heard in the high singles to low teens against Dated Brent, FOB, several trade sources said.
Meanwhile, Chevron moved its early-Dec loading barrels of Australia's Wheatstone condensate to ExxonMobil, market sources said. However further trade details remain unconfirmed, according to a Singapore-based crude trader. Chevron's late-Dec loading Wheatstone condensate cargo however, remains unsold, the crude traders added.
Valuations for Dec-loading Ichthys Field Condensate were at premiums in the mid-teens/barrel to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, CFR South Korea, while Dec-loading barrels of Wheatstone condensate was valued at a premium in the low teens/barrel to the same benchmark, according to a Singaporebased crude trader.
Additionally, valuations of Dec-loading barrels of the Australian North West Shelf condensate cargo, were in a range of premiums in the high-singles/barrels to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, CFR South Korea, according to a trade source.
Elsewhere, a Middle Eastern producer moved Nov-loading

barrels of Khuff condensate to another Middle East refiner, via a tender, however price levels of the trade remain unclear. Another Middle East trading hous,e meanwhile, was heard to have offered Nov-loading barrels of Qatar's Low Sulfur Condensate and Deodorized Field Condensate to several Korean end-users, after having been awarded the barrels in a sell tender by QatarEnergy earlier in the month, market sources said.
Over on the heavy sweet crude front, Petrolines for Crude Oil Co. Ltd., sold 600,000 barrels of Nile Blend crude cargoes - for loading over early December -- to Vitol, at a discount of $7s/b to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, FOB, according to a trade source close to the matter.
On the tender front, Pertamina recalled an earlier issued buyterm tender for various crude and condensate grades for loading over January to March 2027, according to several Singaporebased crude trader sources, to their refineries in Balikpapan, Balongan, Cilacap and Tuban. The tender was initially set to close on Nov. 2, with validity until Nov. 6.
On the upstream crude oil futures front, diplomatic talks on the war in the Middle East brought a measure of calm to the market, weighing on the front-month ICE Brent crude futures contract.
Representatives from Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and Turkey met with Iranian leaders in Tehran on Oct. 26 to discuss the ongoing supply crisis, according to media reports.
"Renewed Middle East diplomacy is seeking to avert wider US-Iran escalation, with Saudi, Pakistani, and Turkish officials meeting Iranian leaders to revive talks and prevent further USIsraeli strikes. The effort comes before US midterms, as Trump hints strikes could resume," said Saxo APAC Research Oct. 27.

Platts Mediterranean & Black Sea Sweet Crude Daily Commentary
CPC Blend, sweet complex remains under pressure
Nov Saharan Blend OSP at $5.75/b over Dated Brent
CPC Blend cargoes continued to lead declines in the Mediterranean crude oil market Oct. 27 as competing barrels pressured an already oversupplied market.
Weak activity was heard in the region early in the session, with indicative bids in the CPC Blend market heard significantly lower than the previous value.
The physical market remained in a contango with December loading volumes yielding higher premiums, although they were capped by forthcoming WTI Midland volumes.
"December perhaps can be slightly better, but with WTI [Midland] now on offer, the whole complex is going lower," one Black Sea trader said.
Elevated freight rates had trapped crude barrels in the region as FOB differentials continued to be heard lower on sweet grades as unplaced volumes sought homes beyond the Med basin.
PetroChina offered two CPC Blend cargoes during the Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 27, demonstrating weaker values on the day.
The company offered a cargo of the Kazakh crude loading over Nov. 16-20 at a discount of $9.75/b to Dated Brent, and a Nov. 2024 loading cargo at a discount of $9.25/b to Dated Brent.
Neither offer was able to attract a buyer by the London close.

"Freight is forcing Med [barrels] to clear locally," another Europe-based crude trader said.
Algeria's state-owned Sonatrach has lowered the official selling price for Saharan Blend by $7.25/b for November loading cargoes, according to a notice seen Oct. 27 by Platts.
Sonatrach set its Saharan Blend OSP for September loading at Dated Brent plus $5.75/b.
November loading volumes continued to be heard changing hands at discounts to the OSP.
"The [Med] market is collapsing," said a Med crude trader, noting that a Sarahan Blend cargo loading in the second half of November was traded at a discount to Dated Brent.
The regional sour crude market was also under pressure from excess supply with ample barrels in the region.
While November-arriving Iraqi Basrah Medium volumes were heard to be largely placed, cargoes arriving in December were heard at a discount to Calendar December Dated Brent.
"Basrah and Kirkuk are still available," said one Med sour crude trader.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Ernest Puey
Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
North Sea under pressure as supply balance lengthens rapidly
Unfavourable freight economics trap barrels within Europe
North Sea crude differentials were under pressure Oct. 27 as November barrels struggled to find homes amid a well-supplied market, exacerbated by a lack of arbitrage opportunities out of Europe due to punitive freight rates.
"Freight has made the Atlantic basin locked, most of it needs to clear locally," a Europe-based North Sea crude trader said, pointing to a long European supply balance for the second half of November.
Availabilities for local North Sea basket grades were heard clustered within the third decade of November, although traders noted that activity had been thin in recent sessions.
The recent weakening in values across adjacent regions continues to pressure basket grades, with the Mediterranean's CPC Blend cited as weighing on differentials for the North Sea's Forties.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process saw two November-loading cargoes of Oseberg offered by Equinor, both left outstanding at the London close at premiums as low as $7.95/barrel over Dated Brent without finding a buyer.
Traders were also pegging end-November values for grades, including Forties and Ekofisk, lower during European afternoon trading, with Forties in particular heard below a $3/b premium to Dated Brent.
With end-November arrivals of WTI Midland pointing to a wellsupplied European environment, values had been under pressure,

Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Freight costs remain painful for buyers: trader
Angola to load 33 cargoes in Dec: finalized program
Trading pace in the West African crude market remained sluggish Oct. 27, amid traders highlighting a cycle-to-cycle lull in spot activity, with market fundamentals remaining difficult for cash differentials.
"It's very slow and it's quite a depressed market," said one Europe-based WAF crude trader to Platts on the day. "Refinery margins are thinning and China is still too quiet on the buy side. Meanwhile, freight remains a pain but sellers basically just have to deal with it at this point."
Indeed, the trader said that while freight costs, one of the key drivers in softer prices in recent cycles, were showing signs of coming down from their highs, the moves were not doing too much to "alleviate the pain."
That being said, there were signals of at least some Eastern demand in recent days with cargoes of WAF crude heard sold into Asia, including the grades Dalia, Djeno and Mandji, market sources said.
The collection of December lifting schedules in West Africa continued Oct. 27. A copy of Angola's finalized loading program for the month was seen by Platts on the day, showing some 1.025 million barrels/day scheduled.
The program consisted of 33 cargoes, up by one from the provisional program circulated to market participants Oct. 19. The cargo added was one of Pazflor, set to be lifted by state oil company Sonangol.
Nigerian crude Bonny Light will load 11 cargoes in December, a copy of the grade's loading program showed on the day. Average loadings for Bonny have been provisionally set at 305,419 b/d.

Asian refiners view US crude premium as cost of stable supply, logistics security
Some refiners pay premiums above $20/b for WTI Midland Japan's US crude imports surge 352% YOY WTI Midland at discount to Murban on CFR Asia basis
East Asia continues to rely heavily on US crude as a leading non-Middle Eastern supply source amid efforts to diversify procurement, with double-digit premiums for WTI Midland and West Texas Light delivered into Asia potentially reflecting the value of enhanced supply reliability and logistical security, according to industry sources and government ministries during Oct. 22-27.
For Thailand, Japan, South Korea and Taiwan, US crude has been the leading and most reliable non-Middle Eastern supply option, feedstock managers at Japanese, South Korean, Taiwanese and Thai refiners in Negishi, Ulsan, Xiaogang and Rayong, as well as Singapore-based traders, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, during Oct. 22-27.
Refiners' trading teams have struggled to secure better spotmarket offers, with WTI Midland premiums consistently exceeding $10/b since the start of the second quarter; however, given the irregular availability of medium and heavy sour crude from the Middle East, elevated Persian Gulf-Far East freight and insurance costs, and heightened shipping security risks, the premium for US crude may reflect the value of more reliable delivery and supply, the Japanese, South Korean, Taiwanese and Thai refinery feedstock managers said.
Platts assessed WTI Midland on a DES Yeosu basis at a premium of $34.60/b to front-month Dubai on Sept. 18, marking a record-high price differential for the light, sweet US crude delivered to the Far East. The premium was last assessed at $26.30/b on Oct. 26.

PrairieSky Royalty sees growing interest in WCSB crude oil plays
Third quarter output rises 5% to 26,936 boe/d
Spotlight on Clearwater, Mannville, Duvernay
Resurgence anticipated in light crude oil plays
PrairieSky Royalty Ltd. anticipates continued development of leasing and drilling activity in heavy and light crude oil plays in the Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin, driven by high commodity prices and a growing competitiveness of the basin, CEO Andrew Phillips said Oct. 27.
"Organic growth continues in our business," Phillips said on a webcast to discuss the company's third-quarter earnings. "Strong leasing activity continues as PrairieSky entered into 49 new lease arrangements with 41 oil companies. New leasing was pervasive across our entire land base, and 230 wells were spud in Q3."
"Our team continues to execute well and is focused on continued active leasing of our large mineral title position. With the capital cycle turning and a number of teams restarting, grassroots exploration in the basin has returned for the first time since 2013 and 2014. We are uniquely positioned to capitalize on this trend," he said.
His statements came after PrairieSky announced late Oct. 26 that total crude oil, NGL and natural gas production last quarter from its WCSB operators was 26,936 barrels/day of oil equivalent, a 5% growth compared with 2,687 boe/d in the third

quarter of 2025. Of the total Q3, 2026 output, crude oil volumes averaged
14,739 b/d, followed by NGL at 2,647 b/d and natural gas at 57 million cubic feet/day, it said, noting the royalty owner benefited from a crude oil price of C$100.15 ($71.27)/b last quarter and an operating net back of C$58.47/boe.
"The Clearwater, Mannville Stack and [West Shale] Duvernay plays are the biggest contributors to these volume increases," CFO Pam Kazeil said on the same webcast. "Royalty production revenue totaled C$153.4 million in the quarter compared to C$107.7 million in Q3 2025."
Clearwater heavy oil royalty production averaged over 3,200 b/d last quarter, an increase of 25% over Q3 2025, with elevated levels of third-party activity and an estimated 60% of production supported by waterflood operations, mitigating declines in the play, PrairieSky said in its earnings release, adding it anticipates continued growth in royalty production into 2027.
Activity in the Mannville Stack play included a record 25 well spuds in the quarter, with heavy oil royalty production averaging more than 1,150 b/d, while year-to-date 2026 Duvernay royalty production has increased 37% to about 1,500 boe/d, it said.
Strong drilling activity Last quarter, production volumes were also positively
impacted by the ramp-up of thermal oil volumes, PrairieSky said.
Drilling activity was strong in Q3 with a record 30 Duvernay spuds in the quarter, bringing the total for the year to 81, including 65 in the prolific West Shale Basin, Vice President of Geosciences and Capital Markets Mike Murphy said on the same webcast.
"As of the end of this quarter, only 22 of these West Shale [Duvernay] wells were on production," Murphy said. "So, upcoming completions are expected to continue to support Duvernay light oil growth through Q4 [the current quarter] and early 2027. Notably, we've seen a resurgence in other light oil plays in the basin, including short-cycle time plays like the Viking, where our spud count is up 49% year-to-date."
Also, waterflood operations continue to expand into Clearwater, with an estimated 60% of the company's production in the play under waterflood support, supporting low declines and a highly sustainable production base, Murphy said.
PrairieSky is also anticipating growth in the longer-term period of 2030 to 2040 to come from Western Saskatchewan, where it sees the potential to add 4,500-4,500 b/d of royalty barrels, Phillips said.
Lastly, growth is also seen in the conventional plays where new technological advancements and multilateral drilling are opening up new opportunities for incremental production, Phillips said.
"We've been very actively leasing a lot of conventional plays that had been quite quiet over the last five years," Phillips said.
-- Ashok Dutta

Arctic Gateway signs deal with Dubai to develop Canada's Port of Churchill
CentrePort Canada also a signatory
Group eyes integrated transportation corridor
Manitoba-based Arctic Gateway Group has signed an agreement with DP World and CentrePort Canada Inc. to develop growth opportunities at the Port of Churchill that include the construction of infrastructure to export multiple commodities - crude oil, LNG, hydrogen, grain, zinc, potash, and uranium - to global markets from the Canadian Arctic Ocean.
The deal focuses on port operations, logistics services, and the potential to develop an integrated transportation corridor linking the Port of Churchill, the Hudson Bay Railway, CentrePort Canada, and DP World's global logistics network, AGG said late Oct. 26.
DP World is part of Dubai Ports World and specializes in port and terminal operations globally.
AGG owns and operates the Port of Churchill, the Hudson Bay Railway, and the Churchill marine tank farm and has a mandate to further develop facilities at the Port of Churchill with private sector investment, it said.
At present, the port is shipping a range of commodities, including critical minerals, grain, and potash, AGG said.
Manitoba's key inland and coastal ports are poised for even further growth, AGG said, including the potential to connect Europe and Asia intermodally through Canada, thereby establishing new trade options.
The Port of Churchill provides direct access to Europe, the Middle East, Africa, and South America via the Atlantic, without the need to build a pipeline across the Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence River, AGG said.
Year-round shipping In early September, AGG said studies indicated the feasibility
of maintaining year-round operations at the Port of Churchill and exporting additional commodities.
"Our next steps are focused on the practical work required to scale the corridor: consulting with rights holders, determining the appropriate modernization of the port, advancing the railway upgrade, continuing marine planning and validation, and working with governments, Indigenous partners, customers, and private investors on the investments required," AGG CEO Chris Avery told Platts then.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. "This season [July to October] is already demonstrating that commercial opportunity," Avery said. "Churchill is experiencing the most diversified shipping season in its history, including the return of grain exports for the first time since 2020." The port is also handling northern resupply, zinc concentrate, and its first-ever export shipment of Manitoba-produced potash, he said, noting VLCC loadings of crude oil and LNG exports, too, are in the cards.
-- Ashok Dutta

Gasoline
Indonesia seeks China, India gasoline supply, eyes long-term deals
Jakarta pivots to Asian suppliers for fuel
Geopolitical risks force supply chain shift
Domestic stocks fall below 20-day threshold
Indonesia is seeking gasoline supplies from China and India and pursuing long-term contracts in a bid to diversify fuel procurement amid disruptions to energy supply chains, Energy and Mineral Resources Minister Bahlil Lahadalia said Oct. 26.
"I will travel to several countries, including India and China, to secure certainty of supply," Lahadalia told reporters at a press conference in Jakarta, adding that the government had been instructed to secure long-term supply contracts.
Indonesia has cut gas exports to Singapore's Gas Supply Pte Ltd. and Sembcorp by 31 million standard cubic feet/day, starting Oct. 24, to meet domestic power demand. Indonesia said the reduction does not constitute a breach of contract as it accounts for only 8% of the total contract. The reduction impacts less than 2% of Singapore's gas demand, Singapore's Minister for Energy, Trade and Industry Tan See Leng said Oct. 26.
As one of the main fuel suppliers, Singapore provided more than 57% of Indonesia's gasoline and blendstock imports in 2025, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. As of Oct. 27, Singapore's gasoline and blendstocks discharged into Indonesia stood at 134,500 barrels/day this year, accounting for over 66% of Indonesia's total imports, the CAS data showed.
Indonesia has been facing mounting pressure on its fuel procurement. Fuel import tenders have attracted few or no bidders amid disruptions linked to the Middle East conflict, forcing state-owned Pertamina to seek alternative suppliers outside its normal procurement channels, the Energy and Mineral Resources Ministry said Oct. 5.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed 92 RON unleaded gasoline FOB Singapore cargo at $140.37/barrel Oct. 26, up $3.23/b over the day.
'Survival mode' Lahadalia described Indonesia's approach as being in "survival
mode" and said President Prabowo Subianto had instructed the government to prepare measures to maintain supply.
"We know that the geopolitical situation remains uncertain, and we cannot predict or analyze when the ongoing tensions in the Middle East will end. Therefore, at the direction of President Prabowo, the Indonesian government has instructed us to immediately take precautionary measures and prepare alternatives, including the worst-case scenarios, to ensure access to energy sources," Lahadalia said.
Despite seeking alternative suppliers and long-term contracts, Lahadalia said domestic fuel stocks remained safe. "Our fuel stocks remain above the national minimum requirement.

Subsidized fuel prices will not increase, and I have been instructed to secure long-term supply contracts," he said.

The government is also considering increasing the Pertalite quota following higher consumption as motorists switch from non-subsidized gasoline, Lahadalia said Oct. 23.

SOUTH KOREA DATA: Light distillate stocks fall on month to 23.764 mil barrels in Sept
Gasoline stocks fall 4.1% on month in Sept
Naphtha imports climb 24.50% from August
LPG demand increases 0.728% on month
South Korea's stocks of light distillates, including gasoline, naphtha and LPG, fell 4.35% month over month and fell 8.46% year over year to 23.764 million barrels in September, data from state-run Korea National Oil Corp. showed Oct. 26.
Gasoline stocks fell 4.1% month over month, but rose 9.7% year over year, to 5.842 million barrels in September amid higher exports and increasing domestic demand, the data showed.
The country's gasoline exports edged up 0.50% month over month, and fell 1% year over year, to 9.067 million barrels in September, according to the data.
South Korea's gasoline demand rose 6.0% month over month and 2.0% year over year to 8.885 million barrels in September, the KNOC data showed.
Gasoline production fell 1.9% month over month and 2.7% year over year to 15.797 million barrels, the data showed.
As part of efforts to cushion the impact of rising pump prices, the South Korean government has implemented larger tax cuts on diesel and gasoline beginning on March 27, which have effectively brought down pump prices.
The government has decided to extend its auto fuel tax cut for an additional two months through the end of November amid renewed conflicts in the Middle East and concerns about disruptions to transit through the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait.
The government has also maintained a fuel price cap scheme it employed on March 13, under which the government sets maximum prices for gasoline, diesel and kerosene so as to keep pump prices from sharply rising.

Naphtha import rebounds South Korea's naphtha stocks edged 3.1% higher month
over month and 3.4% year over year to 14.826 million barrels in September, attributed to higher import volumes and a decline in exports.
Naphtha imports climbed 24.50% from August but fell 47.80% from a year earlier to 11.452 million barrels in September.
Domestic naphtha production was down 3.7% month over month and increased 9.7% year over year at 24.322 million barrels.
September's naphtha consumption declined 1% from August and 11.5% year over year to 31.777 million barrels, marking a seventh consecutive year-over-year decline amid the prolonged downturn in the country's petrochemical industry. Weak cracker economics and persistent pressure on downstream petrochemical margins have continued to weigh on naphtha feedstock demand.
Exports also fell sharply to 557,000 barrels in September, compared with 970,000 barrels in August and 2.705 million barrels a year earlier, leaving more barrels in the domestic market. The government has imposed export controls on naphtha since March 27, as domestic supply has become increasingly tight.
LPG stocks slump South Korea's LPG stocks fell 50.82% year over year, extending
a downtrend that began in January, to 3.096 million barrels in September, the lowest volume so far this year, the KNOC data showed. On a month-over-month basis, the country's LPG stocks fell 29.06%, continuing a decline that started in August.
The drop in LPG stocks came on the back of a 6.84% monthover-month decline in LPG output to 2.274 million barrels in September and a 3.18% fall in imports from August.
South Korea's LPG demand for transportation and industrial purposes rose a marginal 0.728% from a month earlier to 11.065 million barrels in September, extending an uptrend that began in June, the KNOC data showed. On a year-over-year basis, consumption slipped 5.395%.
-- Zoey Ng, Joshua Ong, Norazlina Jumaat, Charles Lee

The recent strength in prices was primarily attributed to limited product availability ahead of the start of the new crushing season.
"Most distilleries in Pakistan have shut down due to the unavailability of molasses. Fresh molasses supplies are expected to arrive from end November," said Kamran Zafar, General Manager at JDW Group.
Pakistan's voluntary blending of up to 5% ethanol into gasoline has supported higher domestic demand amid elevated fuel prices following the recent Middle East conflict, Zafar added.
Meanwhile, exporters also reported steady buying interest from key destination markets, further underpinning sentiment.
Karachi-based market participants said inquiries continued to emerge from Europe and several destinations in East Asia, lending additional support to prices despite elevated freight costs.
Market participants are also closely monitoring price movements in competing origin markets, particularly Brazil.
"Supplies are limited in Pakistan, and sellers do not have sufficient stocks available," a Karachi-based exporter said. "At the same time, buyers are watching price movements in Brazil for both hydrous and anhydrous ethanol. Although inquiries from Europe continue, higher freight costs are weighing on purchasing decisions, and some buyers are waiting for a clearer pricing direction from Brazil, where trade flows remain more economical."
The exporter added that demand from Far East Asia remains a key focus for Pakistani suppliers, as the origin continues to maintain a competitive edge over several alternative suppliers.
The country's ethanol exports fell 10.4% month over month to 50.4 million liters in September, according to the Pakistan government's Trade Information Portal.
Looking ahead, market participants expect supply tightness to continue supporting prices in the near term. Pakistan's crushing season is expected to begin toward the end of November, with peak ethanol production typically occurring between February and March, which could ease supply constraints later in the marketing year.

Platts European Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
European gasoline prices drop on oversupply

Weak demand limits blending activity incentives
European gasoline markets weakened on Oct. 27 as ample regional supply and limited demand combined with elevated freight costs to restrict outlets for barrels, while deteriorating

gasoline-naphtha blending economics added further pressure. Platts assessed FOB ARA Eurobob gasoline barges at $1,085/
metric ton on Oct. 27, down $24.50/mt, while the equivalent cash differential widened $7/mt to $29/mt.
Market sources described Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp as well supplied, with high freight costs limiting the ability to move barrels between regions or clear surplus product through export arbitrage. A Europe-based trader said there was "not much gasoline tightness at the moment," with ARA well supplied, adding that fundamentals pointed to further downside after cash differentials had previously strengthened sharply.
Freight remained a key constraint on product mobility. The trader said the US was not currently pulling European cargoes amid stockbuilding, while elevated freight costs were further restricting arbitrage opportunities. Against this backdrop, FOB prices needed to fall to levels that could clear product, the trader said.
Coastal flows into Northwest Europe were also constrained, particularly for smaller cargoes. A second Europe-based trader said freight was "a major problem for small cargoes into the UK," while limited recent coaster activity left delivered values difficult to establish. Rhine logistics provided a further constraint on moving barrels away from ARA, with a market source saying product movements along the river were severely restricted.
Demand was also described as weak. One market participant said there was "really lots of supply and no demand," while a Europe-based broker similarly noted that there were "not many bids flying around." Tendering for next year's supply was also described as limited, although a third Europe-based trader said it remained early for such activity.
Meanwhile, gasoline-naphtha economics were described as weakening, reducing the incentive to blend naphtha into the gasoline pool. The broader blending picture remained mixed, however, with expensive MTBE continuing to support nonoxygenated blending economics, according to a market source.
In the Mediterranean Basin, product remained on offer amid subdued demand, with two 25,000 mt gasoline cargoes offered during the Platts Market on Close assessment process. A Mediterranean-based trader said high freight was similarly an obstacle to arbitrage barrels out of the region, although freight economics could favor moving product from the Mediterranean rather than ARA, depending on the Med/North spread.
Platts assessed FOB Med gasoline cargoes at $1,108.75/mt on Oct. 27, down $38.75/mt, with the equivalent cash differential falling $6.75/mt to $30.25/mt on the day.

The November/December spread fell $4.50/mt to $36/mt, while December/January fell $4.25/mt to $20.75/mt.
The November Med/North gasoline differential, the spread between the November FOB Med swap and the equivalent

FOB ARA Eurobob barge swap, fell 50 cents/mt to a $22.50/mt Mediterranean premium, while the December spread rose $1/mt to a $22/mt premium.

Jet
November-December European jet fuel-LSGO differential shifts to contango
Positive jet/diesel regrade incentivizes production
Arbitrage flows ease prompt supply tightness
The European jet fuel structure shifted from backwardation to contango on Oct. 26 amid ample prompt jet fuel supply, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, and market sources.
The differential between the November Jet CIF Northwest Europe price and Intercontinental Exchange Inc. low sulfur gasoil narrowed from $137.25/metric ton on Oct. 23 to $117.25/mt on Oct. 26, Platts data showed. The December contract narrowed to a lesser extent, from $137.75/mt on Oct. 23 to $131/mt, reflecting a shift in market structure to a contango of $13.75/mt.
Market participants attributed the shift in the swaps curve to relatively ample prompt jet fuel supply compared with other middle distillates, adding downward pressure to November contract prices.
Tight physical supply and high freight rates associated with uncertainty regarding a resumption of normal trade through the Strait of Hormuz and Bab al-Mandab Strait have further supported prices down the curve, which remains backwardated.
The jet/diesel regrade remained positive for an extended time, incentivizing refiners to maximize jet production, alleviating pressure on prompt jet supply. Platts assessed the regrade at $1.56 on Oct. 23.
Jet fuel demand is also seasonally weak, leading to less pressure on stocks in November compared to December, as demand is expected to pick up for the European winter holiday season.
"Low [exchange of futures for swaps] has made arbitrage significantly more attractive," a source familiar with the matter reported, further softening jet fuel differentials.
"Jet premiums have come off aggressively, and we have been hearing traders stepping in to supply the market, buying off refiners in Singapore and selling into Europe," the source added.
The physical European jet market remains tight as freight rates have pushed up prices across the entire middle distillate complex. ICE LSGO futures contracts remain backwardated alongside jet fuel outright prices, with the M1/M2 Jet CIF NWE outright time spread at $43 on Oct. 26, according to Platts data.
-- Oskar Leskow

Water levels on Rhine rise, but relief forecast to be short-lived
South Korea's Sep jet fuel exports fall 5.8% YOY to 8.29 mil barrels
The Asian jet fuel/kerosene complex market structure dipped slightly Oct. 27 amid the tail-end of the South Korean spotloading program, while market participants await confirmation of China's November refined products export plan.
Market sources said that China's export quotas for November will likely be allocated this week and are expected to decline. Five of the six Chinese refiners who spoke to Platts said they would cut their utilization in November, capping production, as they expected to make losses due to high freight and insurance costs.
Brokers pegged the front-month November-December FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene swap time spread -- an indicator of the near-term market outlook -- at plus $1.05/b in midmorning Asia trading Oct. 27, narrowing from the Platts assessment of plus $1.09/b at the Oct. 26 Asian close.
In the physical segment, still-weak fundamentals continued to exert downward pressure on the cash differential. Platts assessed the FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene cargo cash differential at minus 45 cents/b to the Mean of Platts Singapore jet fuel/kerosene assessments at the Oct. 26 Asian close, down 27 cents/b day over day.
Elsewhere, water levels on the Rhine, a critical European inland waterway for freight, rose above 50 centimeters Oct. 26 and are forecast to hover around 70 cm in the next few days, but the rebound in flows is expected to provide only short-term relief to fuel supply chains across the region, according to market participants.
The Rhine water levels exceeded 50 cm on Oct. 26 for the first time since Sept. 4, with levels mostly below 10 cm from Sept. 24 to Oct. 10 and mostly below 20 cm from Oct. 11 to Oct. 22.
A market participant said the latest rise is unlikely to provide meaningful relief, as the window for barges to pass the Maxau area is expected to be too short to materially ease logistical constraints. Conditions are particularly tight in Switzerland, according to the source, where product inventories are extremely low and market participants are focused on maintaining supplies to retail stations.
In data news, South Korea's jet fuel exports fell 5.8% year over year and 18.2% month over month to 8.29 million barrels in September, data released by state-run Korea National Oil Corp. showed Oct. 26. This marked the first annual decline since April 2026, according to historical KNOC data.
The country's jet fuel output rose 19.9% year over year but slipped 9.8% on the month to 13.87 million barrels.
On the demand front, jet fuel consumption plunged 87.4% year over year and 37.2% month over month to 426,000 barrels in September, marking nine straight months of decline. Demand

over January-September 2026 stood at just 4.95 million barrels, compared with 29.78 million barrels a year ago.
The jet fuel surcharge for Korean Air's tickets rose in September, with medium-haul flights standing at 153,000 won, up from 99,200 won in August and 139,200 won in July, according to the carrier's website. Korean Air will further increase the surcharge to 162,400 won in October and 185,500 won in November, according to its website.
-- Shu ling Lee
Platts European Jet Daily Market Analysis
Freight costs surge is still main concern: sources
Middle East refinery outages reach 1.44 mil b/d
European jet fuel prices were mixed Oct. 27, with strong bidding in the Platts Market on Close assessment process reflecting tightness in the cargo market.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Jet CIF NWE Cargo price at $1,583.25/metric ton, down $69.75/mt day over day. The Jet FOB FARAG barge price also decreased $69.75/mt, closing at $ 1,597.25/mt
The ICE LSGO November contract fell by $59.00/mt to $1,462.75/mt, with November/December backwardation narrowing $3.50/mt to $61.50/mt.
In the jet paper market, the CIF NWE cargo premium over ICE LSGO was assessed at $108.25/mt, down $9.00/mt day over day.
Market participants noted that discussions continued to focus on exceptionally high freight costs.
"Demurrage rates at EU ports surged sharply, driven primarily by exceptionally high freight costs," a trader source said. Another market participant confirmed that elevated freight costs remain a significant concern for the market.
Freight costs remained high, with Platts assessing Clean Arab Gulf-UK Continent 90,000 metric tons LR2 rates at $228.89/ mt Oct. 27, stable day over day. These freight levels continue to impact the regional supply dynamics.
Middle East Middle East refinery outages averaged about 1.44 million
barrels/day in the week under review and were projected to stay stable through the week ending Oct. 30, with Saudi Aramco's Riyadh refinery expected to run at lower capacity and Saudi Arabia's Jazan refinery still offline as of Oct. 23.
Meanwhile, Kuwait's Mina al-Ahmadi, Mina Abdullah and AlZour refineries also remained at reduced rates Oct. 22-23.
Shipping data showed lower traffic in the Strait of Hormuz and high freight costs alongside continued use of alternative routing and transfer arrangements. According to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, Strait of Hormuz traffic fell to 17 daily transits on Oct. 24-25 from 27 transits on Oct. 23, with the weekly total at 146 Oct. 25, down from 161 in the prior week, and energyvessel count down 15% week over week to 82.

-- Julia Shcheulova

Producers and merchants' positioning remains strongly net short, with 179,388 long contracts versus 266,194 shorts as of Oct. 20 -- a net-short position of 86,806 contracts. Producers have a natural long position in gasoil and hedge it by taking short positions.
During the week, long positions increased by 22,138 and shorts by 21,990, so the net position for producers and merchants improved by only 148 contracts and was effectively unchanged. An increase in both long and short positions indicates a pickup in gross hedging activity, with no change in the group's overall sentiment toward market conditions.
The total number of contracts across all groups purchased for the week increased week over week but remained low, approximately 37.5% below the number traded in January. This comes as market participants continue to report low stock levels, meaning less hedging is required, given lower total product volumes.
The data suggest the rebound in market activity reflects higher volatility rather than a major change in sentiment regarding direction.
-- Oskar Leskow
Russia says fourth diesel export tanker to set sail on Oct. 28
Russia agreed with US on Oct. 9 to some diesel exports
Exports restricted amid attacks on Russian refineries

Diesel
Gasoil traders remain bullish as European supply tightness persists
Managed Money positions remain net long
Total positions reported rise, stopping 6-week draw
Hedging activity increases amid volatility
In the latest ICE Commitment of Traders Report, released Oct. 23, activity from market participants showed a prevailing bullish sentiment amid physical tightness in European markets.
Total long and short positions reported to the exchange were up by 20,050 contracts, reversing direction from six consecutive weeks of declining activity amid increased volatility.
Managed money positioning indicates that speculative investor sentiment toward gasoil remained bullish.
As of Oct. 20, managed money investors held 91,440 long contracts against 21,291 short contracts, resulting in a net long position of 70,149 contracts. This net exposure increased by 444 contracts during the week ended Oct. 24, signaling that general sentiment remains largely unchanged, with investors maintaining a bullish outlook.
Although the weekly shift was relatively small, the net long positioning suggests investors generally expect prices to strengthen amid ongoing physical tightness in European markets.

Russian Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said that a fourth tanker carrying Russian diesel will set sail for global markets on Oct. 28, local news agencies reported Oct. 27.
The supplies come after Russia agreed with the US on Oct. 9 to lift some restrictions on exports that were introduced amid a Ukrainian campaign of attacks on its refineries. Analysts at S&P Global Energy CERA estimate that as of Oct. 27, about 60% or nearly 4 million barrels/day of Russian refining capacity is currently offline.
Novak told local media on Oct. 23 that the first three tankers under the deal are on their way to the east coast of the Atlantic Ocean, and that the main supplier could be Rosneft. Rosneft did not reply to a request for comment on its diesel exports and plans up to the end of 2026.
Novak said that the first supplies could reach the US before the midterm elections on Nov. 3.
"Everything depends on the weather. The average time is 10-14 days across the Atlantic; maybe we will manage," he told Russia's Channel 1.
Novak also said Oct. 23 that Russia could export 500,000 metric tons of diesel in October to the US while fully meeting Russian domestic demand. Novak said Oct. 9 that Russia could export 500,000 mt in November and 1 million mt in December.
The deal has faced opposition from some US lawmakers. A group urged US banks and port authorities not to facilitate Russian diesel transactions and warned of potential criminal and

ARGENTINA DATA: Diesel leads rise in September refined products sales
ULSD sales rise 6.6% year over year
98 RON rises 1.3% year over year
Only 95 RON declines
Argentina's diesel and gasoline sales rose 2% in September from the year-earlier month, led by a 6.6% increase in ultra low sulfur diesel sales, Surtidores said Oct. 27.
Refiners sold 8.83 million barrels of diesel and gasoline in September, up from 8.66 million in the year-earlier month, the local trade news outlet said in a report.
The biggest gainer was ULSD, which shot up 6.6% to 1.56 million barrels in September from 1.46 million a year earlier. Sales of No. 2 diesel increased 6.1% to 2.38 million barrels from 2.24 million over the same period, while 98 RON gasoline rose 1.3% to 1.3 million barrels from 1.29 million.
The only decliner was 95 RON gasoline, which fell 2.2% to 3.59 million barrels from 3.67 million over the same period, according to the data.
The overall decline in sales comes as the economy grows at an estimated 1.5% this year, slower than the 3% previously expected, on stubborn 30% annual inflation expected at the end of the year, according to the Central Bank's market expectations survey (REM).
Additionally, debt escalation has also spiked, as late payments on private debts rose to 12.9% in July from 5.6% a year earlier, led by personal loans and credit cards, according to the latest data from the Association of Argentine Banks.
Argentina's state-run YPF had a 55.5% share of diesel and gasoline sales in September, trailed by Shell at 22%, Pan American Energy's Axion Energy at 12.2% and Trafigura's Puma at 5.6%, Surtidores said.
Argentina has about 560,000 barrels/day of installed refining capacity, but demand generally runs at 500,000-550,000 b/d. All of the crude is supplied domestically from fields that produced a record high of 973,700 b/d in September, but refiners must

import additional supplies of higher-grade products due to a lack of capacity to make them.
-- Charles Newbery
SOUTH KOREA DATA: September middle distillate exports dip 3.2% YOY
Gasoil production drops 5% YOY
Jet fuel demand plunges 87.4%
Export restrictions stay as supply concerns persist
South Korea's middle distillate exports dipped 3.2% year over year and 4.4% month over month to 28.37 million barrels in September 2026, data released by state-run Korea National Oil Corp. showed Oct. 26, as diesel and kerosene export restrictions remained in place.
Gasoil exports were 20.01 million barrels, compared with 20.41 million barrels last year and up 2.4% month over month. Exports in the first nine months of 2026 were down 2.9% year over year to 150.2 million barrels.
Co-distillate jet fuel exports fell 5.8% year over year and 18.2% month over month to 8.29 million barrels in September. This marked the first annual decline since April 2026, according to historical KNOC data.
Jet fuel exports over January-September 2026 rose 10.6% year over year to 77.27 million barrels.
It remains unclear whether the country will loosen gasoil export restrictions that have been in place since March. Seoul recently rejected refiners' requests to ease export controls, emphasizing that maintaining stable domestic supplies and preventing sharp increases in pump prices remained its priorities, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reported earlier this month.
"Oil refiners' production capacity has recently recovered and domestic diesel consumption has declined, we are preparing to reasonably reduce the mandatory domestic supply quota and allow refiners to expand their export volumes," President Lee Jaemyung said on the social platform X Oct. 21.
Production Gasoil production fell 5% year over year and 5.2% month over
month to 28.96 million barrels in September, bringing total output since January 6.9% lower to 244.11 million barrels.
Jet fuel output rose 19.9% year over year but slipped 9.8% on the month to 13.87 million barrels. Refiners produced 115.23 million barrels over January-September 2026, up 7.8% year over year.
The Platts-assessed FOB Singapore 10 ppm sulfur gasoil derivative crack spread to front-month Dubai swap -- a measure of the product's relative strength to the crude it was refined from -- averaged $74.38/b in September, widening from $67.42/b in August and $61.79/b in July.
In comparison, the FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene derivative crack spread to front-month Dubai swap averaged $71.30/b in September, lagging gasoil margins during the month.

Auto fuel demand has been affected since early March by higher crude oil prices, driven by the war in the Middle East.
On Sept. 18, the government extended its auto fuel tax cut for an additional two months through the end of November amid concerns about disruptions to transit through the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait.
South Korea will also maintain the fuel price-cap scheme introduced on March 13, under which the government sets maximum prices for gasoline, diesel and kerosene to limit sharp increases in retail fuel prices.
Jet fuel demand plunged 87.4% year over year and 37.2% month over month to 426,000 barrels in September, marking nine straight months of decline. Demand over January-September 2026 stood at just 4.95 million barrels, compared with 29.78 million barrels a year ago.
The jet fuel surcharge for Korean Air's tickets rose in September, with medium-haul flights standing at 153,000 won, up from 99,200 won in August and 139,200 won in July, according to the carrier's website. Korean Air will increase the surcharge further to Won 162,400 in October and Won 185,500 in November, its website showed.
The benchmark Platts FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene outright price averaged $170.06/b in September, widening from $148.43/b in August and $124.93/b in June.
Separately, gasoil and jet fuel inventories in September stood at 9.83 million and 5.67 million barrels, respectively, up 3.6% and 53% year over year. Jet fuel stocks were also up 27.6% on the month, while gasoil inventories fell 10.5% over the same period.
-- Shu ling Lee, Mei huey Ng, Charles Lee
Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Gasoil Daily Market Analysis
South Korea's GS Caltex sells 300,000 barrels of gasoil for Nov. 21-25 loading
South Korea's gasoil exports rises 5% MOM
Backwardation in the Asian ultra-low sulfur gasoil market firmed Oct. 27, buoyed by a tighter supply outlook from China in November.
Chinese refineries are likely to slash their clean oil product exports in November, led by gasoil, due to tight domestic supplies, while weak refining margins weigh on production, Platts previously reported.
One refinery source stated that the total volume of oil companies allowed to export would fall by 30%, with gasoil volumes down about 40%, though details on the basis of the reductions were not provided.
Brokers pegged the front-month November-December Singapore gasoil swaps time spread at $3.40/b in midafternoon Asian trading Oct. 27, widening from $2.81/b at the 0830 GMT Asian close Oct. 26.

Platts European Diesel Daily Market Analysis
European diesel prices drop $59/mt on day
Russia sends fourth diesel tanker to markets
The European diesel complex weakened Oct. 27, driven by a selloff in the front-month ICE low sulfur gasoil futures contract.
Platts assessed the October ICE LSGO contract down $59/ mt to $1,462.75/mt, following three consecutive days of upward momentum. Movements in gasoil futures mirrored those in crude futures amid diplomatic efforts in the Middle East and persistent volatility across the oil complex.
Representatives from Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and Turkey met with Iranian leaders in Tehran Oct. 26 to discuss the ongoing supply crisis, according to media reports.
"Renewed Middle East diplomacy is seeking to avert wider USIran escalation, with Saudi, Pakistani and Turkish officials meeting Iranian leaders to revive talks and prevent further USIsraeli strikes," Saxo APAC Research Oct. 27. "The effort comes before US midterms," as US President Donald Trump "hints strikes could resume."
Furthermore, increased flows of Russian diesel to the US may be pressuring prices day over day amid hopes of some global resupply.
Russian Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak said a fourth tanker carrying Russian diesel will set sail for global markets

Oct. 28, local news agencies reported Oct. 27. Novak told local media Oct. 23 that the first three tankers
under the deal are on their way to the east coast of the Atlantic Ocean, and that the main supplier could be Rosneft. Rosneft did not reply to a request for comment on its diesel exports and plans up to the end of 2026.
Novak said that the first supplies could reach the US before the midterm elections Nov. 3.
"Everything depends on the weather," he told Russia's Channel 1. "The average time is 10-14 days across the Atlantic; maybe we will manage."
Novak also said Oct. 23 that Russia could export 500,000 metric tons of diesel in October to the US while fully meeting Russian domestic demand. Novak said Oct. 9 that Russia could export 500,000 mt in November and 1 million mt in December.
This move is political rather than economic, Novak said in comments to Vesti.
"From the economic perspective, it's beneficial when Russia supplies global markets," Novak said. "It will lower shortages on the diesel market and prices and is beneficial for consumers and the US population."
Market participants agreed that the move was likely political but hoped it could positively impact product availability.
"I believe it is a political strategy with US midterm elections next Tuesday, but product is in transit, and that is a fact," one Mediterranean diesel trader said.
" It's not much, but it is indeed a good effort considering the fact that Russia is exporting via the Arctic Ocean," the trader added.
Meanwhile, activity in the 10ppm CIF NWE and CIF Med diesel MOC remained muted, possibly signaling low demand across the continent with a high flat price. There were seven bids and two offers in the European diesel MOC Oct. 27, Platts data showed.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Sophia Aung

Red Sea Bunkering resumes VLSFO supply at Djibouti
Djibouti resumes VLSFO supply
Ship arrivals jump 20% on year
Red Sea Bunkering has resumed very low sulfur fuel oil (VLSFO) supply at Djibouti as of Oct. 26, according to an email statement from the company's CEO, Abdi Ismail, seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
"Despite current market difficulties, we are successfully maintaining seamless availability, including low sulfur marine gasoil (LSMGO)," the statement said. "Red Sea Bunkering has commissioned its bunker barge, RED SEA III, to support bunker deliveries in the region."
